Although Westerbaan and Westerbaan provide a universal property for normal CPU maps
\varphi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}
without choosing an embedding of
{{\mathcal{B}}}
into bounded operators on some Hilbert space as we have, our results do not require our maps to be normal nor do we require our domain
{{\mathcal{A}}}
to be a von Neumann algebra. As a result, we provide a universal property for minimal Stinespring dilations for
all

C^{*}
-algebras. Furthermore, our universal property highlights the relationship between morphisms of OCP maps and intertwiners of representations. This will have important implications, which will be discussed in Section
7
.

There is also a close connection between our work and that of categorical quantum mechanics
Abramsky and Coecke 2004
,
Selinger 2007
. Proposition
6.15
below shows that
\mathbf{OCP}({{{\mathcal{A}}}})
and
\mathbf{AnRep}({{{\mathcal{A}}}})
are dagger categories. This fact, together with our Stinespring adjunction, has some interesting consequences such as Theorem
6.13
, which states that there exists a unique minimal morphism of anchored representations between
any
two Stinespring representations of the same OCP map.

Definition 6.14
.

A
∗
-category
(also called a
dagger category
) is a category
\mathcal{C}
together with a functor
{}^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{C}
satisfying

i.

x^{*}=x
for all objects
x
in
\mathcal{C}
,

ii.

(f^{*})^{*}=f
for all morphisms
f
in
\mathcal{C}.

Explicitly, functoriality of
∗
says

\mathrm{id}_{x}^{*}=\mathrm{id}_{x}
for all objects
x
in
\mathcal{C}

and

(g\circ f)^{*}=f^{*}\circ g^{*}
for all composable pairs of morphisms
f
and
g
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. For each morphism

({{\mathcal{K}}},\varphi)\xrightarrow{T}({{\mathcal{L}}},\psi)
of OCP maps on
{{\mathcal{A}}},
set

\Big(({{\mathcal{K}}},\varphi)\xrightarrow{T}({{\mathcal{L}}},\psi)\Big)^{*}:=({{\mathcal{K}}},\varphi)\xleftarrow{T^{*}}({{\mathcal{L}}},\psi),

(6.10)

and for each morphism
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)\xrightarrow{(T,L)}({{\mathcal{L}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)
of anchored representations on
{{\mathcal{A}}},
set

\Big(({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)\xrightarrow{(T,L)}({{\mathcal{L}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)\Big)^{*}:=({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)\xleftarrow{(T^{*},L^{*})}({{\mathcal{L}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W).

(6.11)

Then (
6.10
) is a morphism of OCP maps and (
6.11
) is a morphism of anchored representations. Furthermore,
\mathbf{OCP}({{{\mathcal{A}}}})
and
\mathbf{AnRep}({{{\mathcal{A}}}})
are
∗
-categories with respect to these assignments.

Proof 6.16
.

That (
6.10
) is a morphism of OCP maps
has already been proved in the first part of the proof of Proposition
2.10
.
An argument similar to it shows that
L^{*}
is an intertwiner of representations for (
6.11
).
Furthermore, taking the adjoints of the diagrams in (
3.2
) shows that (
6.11
) is a morphism of anchored representations.
Finally,
\mathbf{OCP}({{{\mathcal{A}}}})
and
\mathbf{AnRep}({{{\mathcal{A}}}})
are
∗
-categories due to the definition and properties of the adjoint of a bounded linear map between Hilbert spaces.

Note that
\mathbf{OpSt}({{{\mathcal{A}}}})
and
\mathbf{PAnRep}({{{\mathcal{A}}}})
are
not

∗
-categories with the same
∗
operation because the adjoint of an isometry need not be an isometry. The adjoint of an isometry is, however, a co-isometry, and hence a partial isometry (cf. Chapter 15 in Halmos
Halmos 1982
).

Definition 6.17
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{I}
be two Hilbert spaces.
The
initial space
of a bounded linear map
L:\mathcal{H}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{I}
is the closed subspace
\ker(L)^{\perp}\subseteq{{\mathcal{H}}}.

The map
L:\mathcal{H}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{I}
is called a
co-isometry
iff
L^{*}
is an isometry.
It is called a
partial isometry
iff
L
is an isometry when restricted to
\ker(L)^{\perp}\subseteq{{\mathcal{H}}}.

The following lemma includes several properties of partial isometries that will be used in this work.

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
L:{{\mathcal{I}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{J}
be a bounded linear map between Hilbert spaces.

(a)

If
L
is a partial isometry, it is an isometry when restricted to its initial space.

(b)

Isometries, co-isometries, projections, and unitary maps are partial isometries.

(c)

The composite of a co-isometry followed by its adjoint is a partial isometry.

(d)

Let
{{\mathcal{H}}}
be another Hilbert space (whose dimension is at least
1
). Then
\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}\hat{\otimes}L:{{\mathcal{H}}}\hat{\otimes}{{\mathcal{I}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{H}}}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{J}
is a partial isometry if and only if
L
is a partial isometry. Here,
\hat{\otimes}
denotes the completed tensor product (cf. Section I.2.3 in Dixmier
Dixmier 1981
).

(e)

If
L:{{\mathcal{I}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{J}
is a partial isometry, then there exists
either an isometry or a co-isometry
U:{{\mathcal{I}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{J}
that agrees with
L
on its initial space.

(f)

If
{{\mathcal{I}}}
and
\mathcal{J}
are finite dimensional with the same dimension and
L:{{\mathcal{I}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{J}
is a partial isometry, then there exists a unitary
U:{{\mathcal{I}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{J}
that agrees with
L
on its initial space.

Proof 6.19
.

Most of these are adequately covered in Halmos
Halmos 1982
, Halmos–McLaughlin
Halmos and McLaughlin 1963
, and Hines–Braunstein
Hines and Braunstein 2010
, with the exception of the forward implication in (
d
). Suppose
\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}\hat{\otimes}L:{{\mathcal{H}}}\hat{\otimes}{{\mathcal{I}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{H}}}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{J}
is a partial isometry. Then

\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}\hat{\otimes}L=(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}\hat{\otimes}L)(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}\hat{\otimes}L)^{*}(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}\hat{\otimes}L)=\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}\hat{\otimes}(LL^{*}L)

(6.12)

by Corollary 3 in Section 127 of
Halmos 1982
and the property of adjoints with respect to the completed tensor product. Hence,
\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}\hat{\otimes}(L-LL^{*}L)=0
, which holds if and only if
L=LL^{*}L
. Thus,
L
is a partial isometry by this same corollary.

Remark 6.20
.

The composite of partial isometries need not be a partial isometry (cf. Section 9.3 in Hines–Braunstein
Hines and Braunstein 2010
).

Partial isometries have a natural partial ordering
as described by Halmos and McLaughlin
Halmos and McLaughlin 1963
. This partial ordering is slightly generalized below to include intertwiners of representations.

Definition 6.21
.

Let
{{\mathcal{H}}}
and
{{\mathcal{I}}}
be Hilbert spaces and let
L:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{I}}}
be a partial isometry. If
M:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{I}}}
is another partial isometry satisfying
M(x)=L(x)
for all
x\in\ker(L)^{\perp},
then
M
is said to be an
extension
of
L
and the notation
L\leqq M
will be used.
If
\pi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
and
\rho:{{\mathcal{A}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{I}}})
are representations of a
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}
such that
L
is an intertwiner from
({{\mathcal{H}}},\pi)
to
({{\mathcal{I}}},\rho)
, then
M
is said to be an
intertwining extension
of
L
iff
M
is an intertwiner and
L\leqq M
. In this case, the notation
L\mathrel{\unlhd}M
will be used when the representations are understood from the context. Whenever the notation
\leqq
or
\mathrel{\unlhd}
is used, it will be understood that the operators being compared are partial isometries.

It is straightforward to check the following.

Lemma 6.22
.

The relations
\leqq
and
\mathrel{\unlhd}
from Definition
6.21
define partial orders on the set of partial isometries from
{{\mathcal{H}}}
to
{{\mathcal{I}}}
and
({{\mathcal{H}}},\pi)
to
({{\mathcal{I}}},\rho),
respectively.

The relationship between
\mathrel{\unlhd}
and morphisms of anchored representations will be described in Lemma
6.24
. But first, we provide a useful result that compares
any
two Stinespring representations of the same OCP map.

Theorem 6.22
.

Given any two Stinespring representations
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)
and
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)
of an OCP map
({{\mathcal{K}}},\varphi)
on a
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}},
there exists a unique partial isometry
L:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{I}}}
such that

i.

({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},L)}({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)
is a morphism of anchored representations,

ii.

L
restricted to
\overline{\pi({{\mathcal{A}}})V({{\mathcal{K}}})}\subseteq{{\mathcal{H}}}
is a unitary intertwiner onto
\overline{\rho({{\mathcal{A}}})W({{\mathcal{K}}})}\subseteq{{\mathcal{I}}},
and

iii.

for any other bounded linear map
M:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{I}}}
satisfying the first condition,
M
agrees with
L
on the intersection of their initial spaces (and hence also satisfies the second condition).

The third condition is a uniqueness condition guaranteeing there exists a unique minimal partial isometry (minimal in the sense of the partial order on partial isometries)
L
for which
(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},L)
is a morphism of anchored representations. This is justified in Lemma
6.24
.

Proof 6.23

(Proof of Theorem
6.13
)
.

By Corollary
6.5
, we have a unique pair of morphisms of anchored representations consisting of isometries of the form

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 87.23903pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-35.87865pt\raise 22.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31946pt\hbox{$\textstyle{({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\varphi},\pi_{\varphi},V_{\varphi})}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-87.23903pt\raise-22.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 33.58159pt\raise-22.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-70.30652pt\raise 6.61389pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63612pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},m_{\pi,V})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-49.33594pt\raise-14.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.0906pt\raise 6.61389pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63612pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},m_{\rho,W})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.34375pt\raise-14.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}.

(6.14)

By Proposition
6.15
,
the composite
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},m_{\rho,W}m_{\pi,V}^{*})}({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)
defines a morphism of anchored representations that restricts to
\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}}
. Since
m_{\pi,V}
and
m_{\rho,W}
are isometries,
L:=m_{\rho,W}m_{\pi,V}^{*}
is a partial isometry by Lemma
6.18
. Therefore,
{{\mathcal{H}}}
and
{{\mathcal{I}}}
can be decomposed as

{{\mathcal{H}}}=m_{\pi,V}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\varphi})\oplus m_{\pi,V}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\varphi})^{\perp}\quad\text{ and }\quad{{\mathcal{I}}}=m_{\rho,W}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\varphi})\oplus m_{\rho,W}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\varphi})^{\perp},

(6.15)

where each term in the direct sums is an invariant subspace under the appropriate actions of
\pi
or
\rho
. To see this for the orthogonal complement, take the dual of (
3.1
).
By construction,

L
restricts to a unitary intertwiner from
m_{\pi,V}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\varphi})
to
m_{\rho,W}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\varphi}).

But by the definition of
m
induced from (
5.40
),

m_{\pi,V}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\varphi})=\overline{\pi(A)V({{\mathcal{K}}})}\quad\text{ and }\quad m_{\rho,W}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\varphi})=\overline{\rho(A)W({{\mathcal{K}}})},

(6.16)

which proves the first two claims.

Now, let
M:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{I}}}
be a bounded linear map satisfying the first condition and let
x=\sum_{i}\pi(a_{i})V(v_{i})\in\pi({{\mathcal{A}}})V({{\mathcal{K}}}).
Then

M(x)=\sum_{i}M\big(\pi(a_{i})V(v_{i})\big)=\sum_{i}\rho(a_{i})M\big(V(v_{i})\big)=\sum_{i}\rho(a_{i})W(v_{i})=L(x)

(6.17)

since
M
is linear, since
M\pi(a)=\rho(a)M
for all
a\in{{\mathcal{A}}},
and because
MV=W
. By continuity,
M
agrees with
L
on
\overline{\pi({{\mathcal{A}}})V({{\mathcal{K}}})},
the initial space of
L.

Lemma 6.24
.

In terms of the same notation as in Theorem
6.13
, set

\mathcal{P}(L):=\left\{{{\mathcal{H}}}\xrightarrow{M}{{\mathcal{I}}}\;:\;L\leqq M\mbox{ and }\;\left(({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)\!\!\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},M)}\!\!({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)\right)\in\mathbf{AnRep}({{\mathcal{A}}})_{1}\right\},

(6.18)

where the bounded linear maps
M
are all understood to be partial isometries.
Then

\mathcal{P}(L)=\left\{{{\mathcal{H}}}\xrightarrow{M}{{\mathcal{I}}}\;:\;L\mathrel{\unlhd}M\right\}.

(6.19)

Proof 6.25
.

The containment
\supseteq
in (
6.19
) holds by the diagram (
3.1
) from Definition
3.1
.
To see the reverse containment,
let
M:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{I}}}
be a partial isometry such that
L\mathrel{\unlhd}M.
Then commutativity of the diagrams

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{K}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{H}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{I}}}}
\scriptstyle{V}
\scriptstyle{W}
\scriptstyle{M}

and

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{K}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{H}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{I}}}}
\scriptstyle{V^{*}}
\scriptstyle{W^{*}}
\scriptstyle{M}

(6.20)

follows from (
6.16
), the decomposition (
6.15
), and the fact that
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},L)}({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)
is a morphism of anchored representations. Finally, the intertwining condition (
3.1
) is included in the definition of
L\mathrel{\unlhd}M.
Hence,
\subseteq
holds as well.

Remark 6.26
.

Although maximal elements for the partial order
\leqq
on partial isometries are either isometries or co-isometries (cf.
Halmos and McLaughlin 1963
), this is not true in general of maximal elements for the partial order
\mathrel{\unlhd}
on intertwining partial isometries.
This point will be addressed in Remark
7.15
.

7
Examples and applications

The purification postulate has been used by Chiribella, D’Ariano, and Perinotti to classify finite-dimensional quantum theories among all operational probabilistic theories (OPTs)
Chiribella et al. 2010
,
Chiribella 2014
. We do not need to review OPTs here, but will instead provide a definition of a purification of a process, our formulation of the purification postulate, and the standard finite-dimensional purification postulate of
Chiribella et al. 2010
. Our version of the purification postulate isolates some key assumptions made by
Chiribella et al. 2010
that are implicit from the tensor network (diagrammatic) perspective. We prove our purification postulate using our Stinespring adjunction and show how it reduces to the standard finite-dimensional one.
We will first use our Stinespring adjunction to reproduce a Gelfand–Naimark–Segal (GNS) adjunction for states
Parzygnat 2018
.

Example 7.1
.

Let
{{\mathcal{K}}}:={{\mathbb{C}}}.
By Example
2.6
, an operator state
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\cong{{\mathbb{C}}}
is a state. Applying
\mathbf{Stine}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
to
\omega
provides an anchored representation
({{\mathbb{C}}},{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},V_{\omega}).
In this case,
{{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}\cong{{\mathcal{A}}}
so that
\mathcal{N}_{\omega}\cong\{a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}\;:\;\omega(a^{*}a)=0\}
under this isomorphism. This agrees with the null-space from the usual GNS construction. Hence, the completion
{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}:=\overline{{{\mathcal{A}}}/\mathcal{N}_{\omega}}
and the representation
\pi_{\omega}:{{\mathcal{A}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega})
agree with the usual GNS Hilbert space and representation. By Example
3.5
,
V_{\omega}:{{\mathbb{C}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
produces a unit vector
\Omega_{\omega}:=V_{\omega}(1)\in{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}
and
V_{\omega}^{*}=\langle\Omega_{\omega},\;\cdot\;\rangle:{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}
. Hence,
\mathrm{Ad}_{V^{*}_{\omega}}:{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
can be identified with its evaluation at
1
and is equivalently described by the state
\langle\Omega_{\omega},\;\cdot\;\Omega_{\omega}\rangle:{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}.
Applying
\mathbf{rest}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
entails
\omega=\langle\Omega_{\omega},\pi(\;\cdot\;)\Omega_{\omega}\rangle
, so that
\omega
has been represented by a pure state. If
({{\mathbb{C}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)
is another anchored representation of
\omega,
set
\Omega:=V(1)\in{{\mathcal{H}}}.
By Corollary
6.5
, there is a unique morphism of anchored representations
of the form

({{\mathbb{C}}},{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},V_{\omega})\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}},m_{\pi,V})}({{\mathbb{C}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)
, where
m_{\pi,V}
is given by

\begin{split}{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}&\xrightarrow{m_{\pi,V}}{{\mathcal{H}}}\\
[a]_{\omega}&\xmapsto{\;\;\hskip 8.19447pt\;\;}\pi(a)\Omega,\end{split}

(7.1)

which agrees with the modification from Construction 5.21 in
Parzygnat 2018
.
The universal property of Stinespring’s adjunction thus reproduces the minimality of the GNS construction in the sense that it reproduces the smallest cyclic representation of
{{\mathcal{A}}}
on which
\omega
can be realized as a pure state.

Remark 7.2
.

The GNS adjunction in
Parzygnat 2018
was defined in terms of a category of pointed representations instead of anchored representations. This remark further explains this slight (and only technical) distinction. It is not needed for the present work but is included for completeness. There are natural transformations

\Sigma:\mathbf{Rep}^{\bullet}\Rightarrow\mathbf{PAnRep}

and
\Upsilon:\mathbf{States}\Rightarrow\mathbf{OpSt}
defined as follows.
First, for every
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}},

the category
\mathbf{Rep}^{\bullet}({{\mathcal{A}}})
has objects

({{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,\Omega)
with
{{\mathcal{H}}}
a Hilbert space,
\pi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})

a
C^{*}
-algebra representation, and
\Omega\in{{\mathcal{H}}}
a unit vector.
A morphism
({{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,\Omega)\xrightarrow{L}({{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,\Xi)
is an
isometric intertwiner of representations such that
L(\Omega)=\Xi.

The category
\mathbf{States}({{\mathcal{A}}}),
on the other hand, is the discrete category of
states on
{{\mathcal{A}}},
i.e. the objects are positive unital linear maps

\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
and there are no non-identity morphisms.
To every
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}},
set

\begin{split}\mathbf{Rep}^{\bullet}({{\mathcal{A}}})&\xrightarrow{\Sigma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}}\mathbf{PAnRep}({{\mathcal{A}}})\\
({{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,\Omega)&\xmapsto{\hskip 8.19447pt}({{\mathbb{C}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V_{\Omega})\\
\Big(({{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,\Omega)\xrightarrow{L}({{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,\Xi)\Big)&\xmapsto{\hskip 8.19447pt}\Big(({{\mathbb{C}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V_{\Omega})\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}},L)}({{\mathbb{C}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,V_{\Xi})\Big).\end{split}

(7.2)

Here,
V_{\Omega}:{{\mathbb{C}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{H}}}
is the map that sends
\lambda\in{{\mathbb{C}}}
to
\lambda\Omega.

It is not difficult to show that
\Sigma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
is a functor.
Also, set

\begin{split}\mathbf{States}({{\mathcal{A}}})&\xrightarrow{\Upsilon_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}}\mathbf{OpSt}({{\mathcal{A}}})\\
\omega&\xmapsto{\hskip 8.19447pt}({{\mathbb{C}}},\tilde{\omega}),\end{split}

(7.3)

where
\tilde{\omega}:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is defined by

{{\mathcal{A}}}\ni a\mapsto\tilde{\omega}(a)=\omega(a)\;\cdot\;,

i.e. multiplication by
\omega(a)
on the Hilbert space
{{\mathbb{C}}}.

Since
\mathbf{States}({{\mathcal{A}}})
has only identity morphisms, this specifies the functor

\Upsilon_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}.

Examples
2.6
and
3.5
show that the functors

\Sigma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
and
\Upsilon_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
are faithful but not full.
The only reason these functors are not full is that
the categories
\mathbf{OpSt}({{\mathcal{A}}})
and
\mathbf{PAnRep}({{\mathcal{A}}})

contain more data in their morphisms. However,
the only added information for a morphism of
states
and
pointed representations
is a phase factor, which is a symmetry that can safely be ignored
in the discussion of the GNS construction.

Given a
∗
-homomorphism
f:{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{A}}},

the equalities

\mathbf{PAnRep}(f)\circ\Sigma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}=\Sigma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime}}\circ\mathbf{Rep}^{\bullet}(f)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{OpSt}(f)\circ\Upsilon_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}=\Upsilon_{{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime}}\circ\mathbf{States}(f)

(7.4)

also hold. Furthermore, in the diagram
10
10

10

The dashed arrows are used to depict the three-dimensional nature of the diagram in (
7.5
). They do not have an alternative mathematical meaning.

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1.5}\lx@xy@tip{-1.5}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.89442pt\raise-0.44722pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern-0.89442pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.0pt\raise-30.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Sigma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.0pt\raise-6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1.5}\lx@xy@tip{-1.5}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.34752pt\raise 1.51497pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\large\phantom{X}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.13892pt\raise 13.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.39168pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Upsilon}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise 39.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1.5}\lx@xy@tip{-1.5}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces}}}},

(7.5)

although the equality

\begin{matrix}\mathbf{rest}\\
\Upsilon\end{matrix}=\begin{matrix}\Sigma\\
\mathbf{rest}\end{matrix}

(7.6)

holds, there is only an invertible modification

\begin{matrix}\Upsilon\\
\mathbf{Stine}\end{matrix}\Rrightarrow\begin{matrix}\mathbf{GNS}^{\bullet}\\
\Sigma\end{matrix}

(7.7)

since the two composites are not
exactly
equal but are canonically isomorphic.
Indeed, for a fixed
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}},
the resulting natural
isomorphism

\textstyle{\mathbf{PAnRep}({{\mathcal{A}}})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{OpSt}({{\mathcal{A}}})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Rep}^{\bullet}({{\mathcal{A}}})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{States}({{\mathcal{A}}})}
\scriptstyle{\Upsilon_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Stine}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{GNS}^{\bullet}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

(7.8)

is defined by its evaluation on a state
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
by the morphism

\big({{\mathbb{C}}},{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega},\pi_{\omega},V_{\Omega_{\omega}}\big)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}},L_{\omega})}\big({{\mathbb{C}}},{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\tilde{\omega}},\pi_{\tilde{\omega}},V_{\tilde{\omega}}\big),

(7.9)

where
L_{\omega}:{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\omega}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{H}}}_{\tilde{\omega}}
is defined as the unique extension of

{{\mathcal{A}}}/{{\mathcal{N}}}_{\omega}\ni[a]\mapsto[a\otimes 1]\in({{\mathcal{A}}}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}})/{{\mathcal{N}}}_{\tilde{\omega}}.

(7.10)

Similar calculations to the above show that this map is bounded and
extends to a unitary intertwiner. Hence,
(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathbb{C}}}},L_{\omega})
defines
an isomorphism in the category
\mathbf{PAnRep}({{\mathcal{A}}}).

The appropriate diagram also commutes when one considers
a
∗
-homomorphism
f:{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{A}}}.

This tells us our Stinespring adjunction reduces to the GNS
adjunction by restricting to the images of
\Sigma
and
\Upsilon.

For example, if
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
is a state, one can construct a pointed representation of it via GNS and view that pointed representation as a preserving anchored representation. Similarly, one can view
\omega
as an operator state and apply Stinespring’s construction to obtain another preserving anchored representation. The invertible modification in (
7.7
) says that these two preserving anchored representations are canonically isomorphic. Working out the details of Stinespring’s adjunction in this present work has highlighted the importance of not restricting morphisms to be isometries so that our universal property is more robust and improves on our previous GNS adjunction in several respects.

Definition 7.3
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra. A
purification
of a state
\omega:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathbb{C}}}
consists of a Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}},
a
∗
-homomorphism
\pi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}),
and a unit vector
\Omega\in{{\mathcal{H}}}
such that

\langle\Omega,\pi(a)\Omega\rangle_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}=\omega(a)\qquad\forall\;a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

(7.11)

Hence, a purification of
\omega
will be written as a triple
({{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,\Omega).

In other words, a purification of a state is Stinespring representation of that state.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{K}}}
a Hilbert space. A
purification
of an OCP map
\varphi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{K}}})
consists of a Hilbert space
{{\mathcal{H}}},
a
∗
-homomorphism
\pi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}}),
and a bounded linear map
V:{{\mathcal{K}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{H}}}
such that

V^{*}\pi(a)V=\varphi(a)\qquad\forall\;a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}.

(7.12)

In other words, a purification of an OCP map is a Stinespring representation of that OCP map.

The following version of the purification postulate might seem unfamiliar, but we show that it is equivalent to the usual purification postulate (from Section VII.A. and VII.B. in
Chiribella et al. 2010
) when the algebras are finite-dimensional matrix algebras. This equivalence is worked out in detail in Lemma
7.5
and Theorem
7.22
.

Postulate 7.13

(The Purification Postulate for processes)
.

Let
{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and
{{\mathcal{K}}}
a Hilbert space. Every OCP map
\varphi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{K}}})
has a purification
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V).
Furthermore, given any other purification
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)
for which
({{\mathcal{H}}},\pi)
is unitarily equivalent to
({{\mathcal{I}}},\rho),
there exists a unitary intertwiner
{{\mathcal{H}}}\xrightarrow{U}{{\mathcal{I}}}
such that
UV=W.

Stinespring’s theorem guarantees the existence of purifications. The existence of the unitary intertwiner in these postulates is referred to as the
essential uniqueness of purifications
.
The purification postulate for processes implies the one for states by setting
{{\mathcal{K}}}={{\mathbb{C}}}.
We will prove the purification postulate for processes on finite-dimensional matrix algebras in Theorem
7.22
and finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras in Corollary
7.13
after a few lemmas.
Our version of the purification postulate is formulated without using traces or tensor products since these may be absent or ambiguous for general
C^{*}
-algebras. This is partially achieved by using completely positive
unital
maps instead of the more common completely positive
trace-preserving
maps. In the finite-dimensional setting, these are equivalent and correspond to the Heisenberg and Schrödinger pictures, respectively. Completely positive unital maps are used to map observables to observables while their duals (adjoints with respect to the Hilbert–Schmidt inner product), completely positive trace-preserving maps, are used to map density matrices to density matrices. However, the category of
C^{*}
-algebras does not have duals and therefore does not have symmetric purifications as defined in
Selby et al. 2018
.
Since states supersede density matrices in the infinite-dimensional setting and the notion of a trace is not always available, it is sometimes more convenient to work within the Heisenberg picture.

Lemma 7.5
.

Fix positive integers
k
and
n
.
Set
{{\mathcal{K}}}:={{\mathbb{C}}}^{k}
, let
\varphi:{{\mathcal{M}}}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{k})\cong\mathcal{M}_{k}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be an OCP map, and let
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)
be a finite-dimensional Stinespring representation of
({{\mathcal{K}}},\varphi).
Then there exists a
p\in{{\mathbb{N}}}
and a unitary map
R:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}
such that

R\pi(A)R^{*}=A\otimes\mathds{1}_{p}\quad\text{ and }\quad\varphi(A)=(RV)^{*}(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{p})(RV)\qquad\forall\;A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}).

(7.14)

In other words, there exists an isomorphism
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},R)}({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p},i,RV)
of anchored representations, where
R
is unitary and where
i
is defined by

\begin{split}i:{{\mathcal{M}}}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})&\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{M}}}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes{{\mathcal{M}}}_{p}({{\mathbb{C}}})\cong{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p})\\
A\;\;\;\;&\xmapsto{\hskip 8.19447pt}\;\;A\otimes\mathds{1}_{p}.\end{split}

(7.15)

Proof 7.6
.

This follows from a general fact about unital
∗
-homomorphisms between finite-dimensional matrix algebras (cf. Section 1.1.2 in Fillmore
Fillmore 1996
) and our definitions.

(7.17)

The map
{{\mathbb{C}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.69989pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-4.13194pt\raise 5.43056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{!}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{A}}}
is the unique unital linear map from
{{\mathbb{C}}}
to
{{\mathcal{A}}}.

Note that the algebras here are finite dimensional
so the tensor product is the standard one. By choosing this convention, the direction of time is up. The direction of time is consistent with
Selby et al. 2018
, while the direction of composition is opposite, because the dual maps on density matrices are used in
Selby et al. 2018
.
Using the shorthand
\mathcal{M}_{n}:=\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
(and similarly for other dimensions), the relationship between our dilation (from Lemma
7.5
) and the one of
(
Chiribella et al. 2010
, Definition 45)
is then given by
