(7.18)

due to Lemma
7.5
.
To prove the purification postulate for processes, we need to recall a few standard facts about the commutant (cf. Chapters 1 and 2 in Dixmier
Dixmier 1981
and Chapter 4 in Fillmore
Fillmore 1996
).

Definition 7.7
.

Let
S\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}
be a subset of a
C^{*}
-algebra
{{\mathcal{A}}}.

The
commutant
of
S
inside
{{\mathcal{A}}}
is
the unital algebra

S^{\prime}:=\{a\in{{\mathcal{A}}}\;:\;as=sa\;\forall\;s\in S\}.

(7.19)

Since the commutant depends on the embedding algebra,

S^{\prime}
will often be written as
S^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}.
11
11

11

Writing
S^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}
will also avoid confusion with
the notation used for different
C^{*}
-algebras
{{\mathcal{A}}},{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime},{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime\prime}
earlier.

Remark 7.8
.

If a subset
S\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}
is
∗
-closed
(meaning
a\in S
implies
a^{*}\in S
), then

S^{\prime}
is a unital
∗
-algebra.
In fact,
S^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{A}}}
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
{{\mathcal{A}}}

since
S^{\prime}=\bigcap_{s\in S}\{s\}^{\prime}
is the intersection of the kernels of the commutators
[s,\;\cdot\;]:{{\mathcal{A}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{A}}},
which are all closed (cf. Chapter 2 in Topping
Topping 1971
).

Example 7.9
.

If
{{\mathcal{H}}}
is a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, then
{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
is
{{\mathbb{C}}}\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}},
all scalar multiples of the identity in
{{\mathcal{H}}}.

If
{{\mathcal{K}}}
is another finite-dimensional Hilbert space, then the commutant
\big({{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})\otimes\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}}\big)^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{K}}})
is
\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}\otimes{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{K}}}),
all bounded operators of the form
{\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{H}}}}\otimes B}
with
B\in{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{K}}}).
If
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
and
R:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}
are as in Lemma
7.5
, then
\pi({{\mathcal{A}}})^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
is
R^{*}\big(\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{p}({{\mathbb{C}}})\big)R,
operators of the form
R^{*}(\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes B)R
with
B\in\mathcal{M}_{p}({{\mathbb{C}}}).
Given a positive integer
t
and non-negative integers
n_{1},\dots,n_{t},c_{1},\dots,c_{t},
with
\sum_{j}n_{j},\sum_{j}c_{j}>0
, set
m=\sum_{j=1}^{t}n_{j}c_{j}
and let

\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{n_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{c_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\Big)

(7.20)

be the
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
consisting of all linear combinations of block diagonal matrices of the form

\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}(A_{j}\otimes B_{j}):=\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}\otimes B_{1}&&0\\
&\ddots&\\
0&&A_{t}\otimes B_{t}\end{bmatrix}

(7.21)

(if any of the integers
n_{1},\dots,n_{t},c_{1},\dots,c_{t},
are zero, terms corresponding to them are excluded from the above matrix).
Then, the commutant of
\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}\big(\mathcal{M}_{n_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes\mathds{1}_{c_{j}}\big)
inside
\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is
\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}\big(\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{c_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}})\big).

The notation
\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits
is used for ‘internal’ direct sum to distinguish it from the ‘external’ direct sum
\bigoplus
.

Theorem 7.9
.

The purification postulate for OCP maps
(Postulate
7.13
) holds whenever all algebras are finite-dimensional matrix algebras and all Hilbert spaces are finite dimensional.

Proof 7.10
.

Purifications exist by Stinespring’s theorem (the associated anchored representation is finite dimensional by minimality). For the essential uniqueness, fix positive integers
k
and
n
.
Set
{{\mathcal{K}}}:={{\mathbb{C}}}^{k}
, let
\varphi:{{\mathcal{M}}}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathbb{C}}}^{k})\cong\mathcal{M}_{k}({{\mathbb{C}}})
be an OCP map, and let
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)
and
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)
be two finite-dimensional Stinespring representations of
({{\mathcal{K}}},\varphi)
with unitarily equivalent representations
\pi:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{H}}})
and
\rho:\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{I}}})
.
By Theorem
6.13
, there exists a partial isometry
L:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{I}}}
such that

({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},L)}({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)
is a morphism of anchored representations.
In what follows, we will prove there exists a unitary
U
such that
L\mathrel{\unlhd}U.
By Lemma
7.5
, there exist integers
p
and
q
together with unitary maps
R:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}
and
S:{{\mathcal{I}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{q}
such that

\begin{gathered}R\pi(A)R^{*}=A\otimes\mathds{1}_{p},\qquad S\rho(A)S^{*}=A\otimes\mathds{1}_{q},\quad\text{ and }\;\\
(RV)^{*}(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{p})(RV)=\varphi(A)=(SW)^{*}(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{q})(SW)\end{gathered}

(7.23)

for all
A\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}}).
Since
\pi
and
\rho
are unitarily equivalent,
p=q
.
These facts imply
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p},i,SW)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},RL^{*}S^{*})}({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p},i,RV)
is a morphism of anchored representations, where
RL^{*}S^{*}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}
is a partial isometry and
i
is the map from (
7.15
).
By Example
7.9
and Lemma
6.18
, there exists a unique partial isometry
P:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}
such that
RL^{*}S^{*}=\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes P
. By the last fact in Lemma
6.18
, there exists a unitary
M:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}
such that
P\leqq M
(cf. Definition
6.21
). Hence
\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes P\leqq\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes M
. By Example
7.9
,
\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes M
is in
i\big(\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\big)^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})\otimes\mathcal{M}_{p}({{\mathbb{C}}}).
Therefore,
\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes P\mathrel{\unlhd}\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes M
with respect to the representation
i
. Hence,

({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p},i,SW)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes M)}({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\otimes{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p},i,RV)
is a morphism of anchored representations by Lemma
6.24
. Setting
U:=S^{*}(\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes M^{*})R,
we obtain the required unitary for essential uniqueness of purifications since it provides an isomorphism

({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{H}}},\pi,V)\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},U)}({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathcal{I}}},\rho,W)

of anchored representations.

The situation in the previous proof is summarized by the commutative diagram

(7.24)

Because
R,U,
and
S
are unitary, commutativity of this diagram is equivalent to the conditions required of morphisms of anchored representations.

}}\quad,

(7.27)

which agrees precisely with Postulate 1 in Section VII.A. in
Chiribella et al. 2010
(after setting
k=1
).

Remark 7.11
.

The unitary
U
in the essential uniqueness of Postulate
7.13
is not necessarily unique if it exists. Indeed, there could be many unitary extensions
\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes M
of the partial isometry
\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes P
from the proof of Theorem
7.22
.

Remark 7.12
.

If one drops the assumptions that the finite-dimensional representations
({{\mathcal{H}}},\pi)
and
({{\mathcal{I}}},\rho)
are unitarily equivalent in Postulate
7.13
and Theorem
7.22
, then one obtains a slight generalization of the purification postulate. In this more general case, there exists an intertwining map
U:{{\mathcal{H}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathcal{I}}}
that satisfies
UV=W
and
U
is either an isometry or a co-isometry. The proof of this is similar to the proof of Theorem
7.22
except that
p
need not equal
q
.
The only partial isometry
O
that satisfies
(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{p})O=O(A\otimes\mathds{1}_{q})
for all
A\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({{\mathbb{C}}})
must be of the form
\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes P
for some partial isometry
P:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{q}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}
. Hence,
one obtains a unique partial isometry
P:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{q}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}
such that
RL^{*}S^{*}=\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes P
.
All intertwining extensions of this partial isometry must therefore also be of this form. Therefore, there exists either an isometry or a co-isometry

M:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{q}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{p}
satisfying
\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes P\mathrel{\unlhd}\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes M
by Lemma
6.18
. Then
U:=S^{*}(\mathds{1}_{n}\otimes M)^{*}R
is the required partial isometry. Note that if
\dim{{\mathcal{H}}}=\dim{{\mathcal{I}}}
is assumed, then the representations are automatically unitarily equivalent since maximal partial isometries between finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces of equal dimension are unitary.

Our formulation of the purification postulate is also valid for arbitrary finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras.

Corollary 7.13
.

The purification postulate for OCP maps
(Postulate
7.13
) holds whenever all algebras are finite-dimensional
C^{*}
-algebras and all Hilbert spaces are finite dimensional.

Proof 7.14
.

Existence follows from Stinespring’s theorem as before. What follows is a proof of the essential uniqueness of purifications.
By the discussions preceding this, it suffices to consider the case

{{\mathcal{K}}}:={{\mathbb{C}}}^{k}\quad\text{ and }\quad{{\mathcal{A}}}:=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}\mathcal{M}_{n_{j}}({{\mathbb{C}}}),

(7.28)

where
t\in{{\mathbb{N}}}
and
n_{j}\in{{\mathbb{N}}}
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}.
Given an OCP map
\varphi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\;\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces{}{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=3.0pt,width=0.0pt}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\!{{\mathcal{B}}}({{\mathcal{K}}}),
it also suffices to consider two Stinespring representations of the form
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m},\pi,V)
and
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m},\pi,W)
, where
m\in{{\mathbb{N}}}
and the representation
\pi:{{\mathcal{A}}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{M}_{m}({{\mathbb{C}}})
is of the form

\pi\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}A_{j}\right)=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}(A_{j}\otimes\mathds{1}_{c_{j}}),

(7.29)

where the
c_{1},\dots,c_{t}
are non-negative integers such that
m=\sum_{j=1}^{t}n_{j}c_{j}.
By similar arguments to those implemented in the proof of Theorem
7.22
, there exist partial isometries (for the non-zero
c_{j}
)
L_{j}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{c_{j}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{c_{j}}
such that
\left(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},L:=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}(\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}\otimes L_{j})\right)
is a morphism of anchored representations from
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m},\pi,V)
to
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m},\pi,W)
. These can be extended to unitaries
U_{j}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{c_{j}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{c_{j}}
by finite dimensionality. Hence,
\left(\mathrm{id}_{{{\mathcal{K}}}},U:=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}(\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}\otimes U_{j})\right)
is an isomorphism of anchored representations.

Remark 7.15
.

If one drops the assumptions that the finite-dimensional representations
({{\mathcal{H}}},\pi)
and
({{\mathcal{I}}},\rho)
are unitarily equivalent in Postulate
7.13
and Corollary
7.13
, then
U=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}(\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}\otimes U_{j})
from the proof of Corollary
7.13
is replaced by an internal block sum of partial isometries that have been extended to isometries or co-isometries. In particular, even though
U
is maximal with respect to
\mathrel{\unlhd}
, it need not be an isometry nor a co-isometry. In more detail, let
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{m},\pi,V)
and
({{\mathcal{K}}},{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n},\rho,W)
be the two Stinespring representations, where

\begin{split}\pi\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}A_{j}\right)=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}(A_{j}\otimes\mathds{1}_{c_{j}}),\quad&\quad\rho\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{t}A_{j}\right)=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}(A_{j}\otimes\mathds{1}_{d_{j}}),\\
m=\sum_{j=1}^{t}n_{j}c_{j},\quad\qquad\;\;&\text{and}\qquad\qquad n=\sum_{j=1}^{t}n_{j}d_{j}.\end{split}

(7.30)

One can show
L
(from Theorem
6.13
) must be of the form
\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}(\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}\otimes P_{j})
.
In fact, all intertwining extensions of
L
must also be of this form.
By extending such intertwining partial isometries, one obtains an isometry or a co-isometry
U_{j}:{{\mathbb{C}}}^{c_{j}}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>{{\mathbb{C}}}^{d_{j}}
if
c_{j}\leq d_{j}
or
c_{j}\geq d_{j}
, respectively.
Hence, if
c_{j}-d_{j}
changes sign as
j
varies, then
U=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{t}(\mathds{1}_{n_{j}}\otimes U_{j})
is neither an isometry nor a co-isometry.

Appendix B
2-categorical preliminaries

We briefly recall the definitions of oplax-natural transformations and modifications.
In addition, we include the universal property associated with adjunctions
because it is used in explaining the Stinespring adjunction more concretely.
For details on 2-categories and their pasting diagrams, we refer the reader to Bénabou’s original work

Bénabou 1967
as well as Kelly and Street’s review
Kelly and Street 1974
.
For a more introductory take emphasizing string diagrams, see
Parzygnat 2019
.
For other details on oplax-natural transformations and modifications, we refer the reader to Section 7.5 in Borceux
Borceux 1994
.

Definition B.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two
(strict)
2-categories and let
F,G:\mathcal{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{D}

be two (strict) functors. An

oplax-natural transformation

\rho
from
F
to
G
, written as

\rho:F\Rightarrow G,
consists of

i.

a function
\rho:C_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>D_{1}
assigning a
1-morphism in
\mathcal{D}
to each object
x
in
\mathcal{C}
in the following manner

\textstyle{x}
\textstyle{F(x)}
\textstyle{G(x)}
\scriptstyle{\rho(x)}
\scriptstyle{\rho}

(B.1)

ii.

and a function
\rho:C_{1}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>D_{2}
assigning
a 2-morphism in
\mathcal{D}
to each 1-morphism
y\xleftarrow{\alpha}x
in
\mathcal{C}
in the following manner

(B.2)

These data must satisfy the following conditions:

(a)

For every object
x
in
\mathcal{C},

\rho(\mathrm{id}_{x})=\mathrm{id}_{\rho(x)}.

(B.3)

(b)

For every pair
(z\xleftarrow{\alpha}y,y\xleftarrow{\beta}x)

of composable 1-morphisms in
\mathcal{C},

the equality

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}.

(B.6)

holds.

Remark B.2
.

We use the prefix “oplax” because for a
lax-natural transformation
(cf. Definition 7.5.2 in Borceux
Borceux 1994
), the source and targets of the 2-morphism in (
B.2
) are switched. Note that Equations (
B.4
) and (
B.6
) must be appropriately modified for a lax-natural transformation. For a
pseudo-natural transformation
, the 2-morphisms in (
B.2
) are (vertically) invertible (cf. Definition
B.5
). The vertical inverse of
\rho(\alpha)
will be written as
\overline{\rho(\alpha)}.
By the uniqueness of inverses, if a pseudo-natural transformation is oplax, then its vertical inverse is lax. This fact is used in Proposition
B.10
. If
\rho(\alpha)
is the identity for all 1-morphisms
\alpha,
then
\rho
is called a
natural transformation
.

The definition of a modification does not
change if one uses oplax-natural
transformations instead of
pseudo-natural transformations.

Definition B.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two
2-categories,
F,G:\mathcal{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{D}

(B.9)

Remark B.4
.

If one has a modification between lax-natural transformations, the diagram in
(
B.9
) is modified appropriately.
This will be used in Proposition
B.10
.

The composition of oplax-natural transformations
and modifications are not changed as a result
of these alterations to the usual definitions.

Definition B.5
.

The
vertical composite
of oplax-natural
transformations is denoted using vertical concatenation as in

(B.10)

(B.13)

and is defined by the assignment

\textstyle{x}
\textstyle{F(x)}
\textstyle{G(x)}
\textstyle{H(x)}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(x)}
\scriptstyle{\rho(x)}
\scriptstyle{\tau(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lambda(x)}
\scriptstyle{m(x)}
\scriptstyle{n(x)}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}m\\
n\end{smallmatrix}}

(B.14)

for each object
x
in
\mathcal{C}.

Notation B.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two (strict) 2-categories. Let

\mathbf{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})

be the 2-category whose objects are (strict) functors from

\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{D},
1-morphisms are oplax-natural transformations,
and 2-morphisms are modifications.
The compositions are defined as above (modifications have one additional composition, which we have not defined).

Definition B.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a (strict) 2-category.
An
adjunction
in
\mathcal{C}

consists of a pair of objects
x,y
in
\mathcal{C},

a pair of morphisms

\textstyle{x}
\textstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}

0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.07622pt\raise 23.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern-25.3418pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.8629pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{g}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1.5}\lx@xy@tip{-1.5}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}.

(B.19)

Conditions (
B.18
) and (
B.19
) are known as the

zig-zag identities
.
An adjunction as above is typically
written as a quadruple
(f,g,\eta,\epsilon)

and we say
f
is
left adjoint

to
g
and write
f\dashv g.

However, the notation

\textstyle{x}
\textstyle{\overset{\mathrm{id}_{x}}{\rotatebox{180.0}{\Large$\circlearrowleft$}}}
\textstyle{y}
\textstyle{\underset{\mathrm{id}_{y}}{\rotatebox{0.0}{\Large$\circlearrowleft$}}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\eta\bot\epsilon}

(B.20)

may occasionally be employed to more clearly indicate all of the data
in the definition of an adjunction.

The usual notion of an adjunction is one where the 2-category is that of
categories, functors, and natural transformations. One may express
adjunctions in terms of a universal property in this case.

Lemma B.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}
be categories, let
F,G:\mathcal{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{D}
be functors,
and let
\eta:\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow G\circ F
and
\epsilon:F\circ G\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}
be natural transformations so that
(F,G,\eta,\epsilon)
is an adjunction. Then, the following universal properties hold.

i.

For every
c\in\mathcal{C},

d\in\mathcal{D},
and
c\xrightarrow{g}G(d)
there exists a unique
F(c)\xrightarrow{f}d
such that

\textstyle{c}
\textstyle{G(d)}
\textstyle{G(F(c))}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{c}}
\scriptstyle{G(f)}

(B.21)

ii.

For every
c\in\mathcal{C},

d\in\mathcal{D},
and
F(c)\xrightarrow{f}d
there exists a unique
c\xrightarrow{g}G(d)
such that

\textstyle{F(c)}
\textstyle{d}
\textstyle{F(G(d))}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{F(g)}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{d}}

(B.22)

Proof B.9
.

This is an equivalent definition of an adjunction (cf. Chapter IV Section 1 in Mac Lane
Mac Lane 1998
). Given
c\xrightarrow{g}G(d),
the morphism
F(c)\xrightarrow{f}d
is given by
f:=\epsilon_{d}\circ F(g).
Conversely, given
F(c)\xrightarrow{f}d
, the morphism
c\xrightarrow{g}G(d)
is given by
g:=G(f)\circ\eta_{c}.

Proposition B.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two (strict) 2-categories.
Let

\textstyle{F}
\textstyle{\overset{\mathrm{id}_{F}}{\rotatebox{180.0}{\Large$\circlearrowleft$}}}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{\underset{\mathrm{id}_{G}}{\rotatebox{0.0}{\Large$\circlearrowleft$}}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\eta\bot\epsilon}

(B.23)

be an adjunction in the 2-category
\mathbf{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}).

Then

\textstyle{F(x)}
\textstyle{\overset{\mathrm{id}_{F(x)}}{\rotatebox{180.0}{\Large$\circlearrowleft$}}}
\textstyle{G(x)}
\textstyle{\underset{\mathrm{id}_{G(x)}}{\rotatebox{0.0}{\Large$\circlearrowleft$}}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(x)}
\scriptstyle{\rho(x)}
\scriptstyle{\eta(x)\bot\epsilon(x)}

(B.24)

is an adjunction in
\mathcal{D}
for all objects
x
in
\mathcal{C}.

Conversely, let
F,G:\mathcal{C}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{D}
be two functors, let

\rho:G\Rightarrow F
be a pseudo-natural transformation, and let

\sigma:\mathcal{C}_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\eta,\epsilon:\mathcal{C}_{0}\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.49588pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.99176pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\mathcal{D}_{2}
be assignments such that (
B.24
) is an adjunction
for all
x
in
\mathcal{C}.
Then there exists an extension of
\sigma
to an oplax-natural transformation for which
\eta
together with
\epsilon
define modifications and (
B.23
) is an adjunction in
\mathbf{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}).
Furthermore,
\sigma
is unique up to a canonical isomorphism.

Remark B.11
.

That
\rho
be a pseudo-natural transformation and not just a lax- or oplax-natural transformation is explicitly used in the proof.

Proof B.12

(Proof of Proposition
B.10
)
.

you found me!

(
\Rightarrow
)
The forward direction was proved in the final Remark in
Parzygnat 2018
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
For the reverse direction,
\sigma_{\gamma}:\sigma_{y}\circ F(\gamma)\Rightarrow G(\gamma)\circ\sigma_{x}
must be constructed for each morphism
y\xleftarrow{\gamma}x
in
\mathcal{C}.
It is cumbersome to do this using globular diagrams, so we implement string diagrams to simplify the proof (see
Parzygnat 2019
for an introduction to string diagrams). By convention, string diagrams will be read from top to bottom and from right to left. Define
\sigma_{\gamma}
by

(B.25)

where
\overline{\rho_{\gamma}}
is the vertical inverse of
\rho_{\gamma}.

To verify the oplax-naturality of
\sigma,
consider a composable pair
z\xleftarrow{\delta}y\xleftarrow{\gamma}x
of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}.

(B.28)

proves condition (
B.6
) for
\sigma
and concludes the proof that
\sigma
is an oplax-natural transformation.
It remains to show that
\epsilon:\mathrm{id}_{F}\Rrightarrow\begin{matrix}[0.6]\sigma\\
\rho\end{matrix}
and
\eta:\begin{matrix}[0.6]\rho\\
\sigma\end{matrix}\Rrightarrow\mathrm{id}_{G}
are modifications.
For this, fix a 1-morphism
y\xleftarrow{\gamma}x
in
\mathcal{C}.

(B.29)

where the last equality follows from the fact that
\overline{\rho}
is the vertical inverse of
\rho.

This proves (
B.9
) for
\eta
, and therefore shows
\eta
is a modification.
A similar proof shows
\epsilon
is a modification. Finally,

\sigma
is unique up to canonical isomorphism by the uniqueness of adjoints in 2-categories (cf. Lemma A.4 in
Parzygnat 2018
).

Remark B.13
.

Proposition
B.10
seems to be a useful fact for adjunctions in 2-categories of functors.
It offers a slightly shorter proof of Theorem
5.5
. One merely has to define the functors
\mathbf{Stine}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
and
\mathbf{rest}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
and define the natural transformation
m_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}
. Then one has to show
\begin{matrix}[0.7]\mathbf{Stine}\\
\mathbf{rest}\end{matrix}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{OCP}}
and prove the zig-zag identities for
(\mathbf{Stine}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}},\mathbf{rest}_{{{\mathcal{A}}}},\mathrm{id},m_{{{\mathcal{A}}}}),
the last of which were essentially tautologies. Proving oplax-naturality of
\mathbf{Stine}
and that
m
is a modification is not necessary thanks to Proposition
B.10
.
